Calamus, the least acrid of the three, requires boiling in several changes of water to reduce its pungency, but the Food and Drug Administration has determined that oil of calamus is carcinogenic, and so the marketing of the oil is prohibited (Larkin 1983). Considering the potential for poisoning and the scarcity of these beautiful wetland wildflowers, I strongly discourage digging up any of these plants for use as food. Indian turnip, jack-in-the-pulpit—Arisaema triphyllum A number of ornamental plants come from this family. The name “dumbcane,” for Dieffenbachia species, refers to the possibility that the unwary nibbler may become unable to speak. Peace lilies, Spathiphyllum species, are highly toxic. The luxuriant plant known as elephant ear (Colocasia esculenta), often used in landscaping in South Texas, grows in abundance along the San Marcos River in Texas. A variety known as taro provides a major root crop in the South Pacific and is the main ingredient in the Hawaiian dish poi (Ellis 1978; Baker 1965). Like other members of the family, taro is toxic raw and must be carefully prepared for food use. For a full list of the common ornamental members of the arum family, see page 267. Family Characteristics Plants of the arum family occur as perennial herbs with thick roots. Arums produce distinctive inflorescences. The inconspicuous flowers, usually white, green, or yellow, are crowded on a succulent dense spike called a spadix. A specialized leafy bract called a spathe often encompasses the spadix. The spathe may be green or colored. ASTERACEAE—COMPOSITE FAMILY, SUNFLOWER FAMILY (FORMERLY COMPOSITAE) Members of the Asteraceae often are herbaceous plants with attractive flowers. A few have caused extensive livestock loss, particularly in cattle and sheep. Though most of the toxic species are distasteful to cattle and sheep, livestock will eat them when other forage is scarce. Humans rarely attempt to use those members of the Asteraceae for food, so for most of those plants human cases of poisoning are unknown. For descriptive information on the family and the edible species, refer to the index. White snakeroot (Ageratina altissima), highly toxic to livestock, has caused human illness and death indirectly through milk produced by cows eating the plants. This milk sickness does not occur throughout the entire range of white snakeroot, but before the twentieth century the disease caused so many human deaths that people were forced to move out of certain areas of the eastern United States. White snakeroot grows throughout the eastern half of North America, into East and Central Texas. Ageratina wrightii, from the Trans-Pecos of Texas to southern Arizona, also is toxic to livestock. Other Ageratina species may also be toxic. One other southwestern plant, jimmyweed (Isocoma pluriflora), found from West Texas to Arizona, has caused milk sickness (Kingsbury 1964; Lampe and Fagerström 1968; Hardin and Arena 1974; Holm et al. 1977). The following southwestern plants also have caused considerable livestock poisoning: the snakeweeds or broomweeds (Gutierrezia texana, G. sarothrae, G. microcephala, and probably others; these were formerly classified as Xanthocephalum), sneezeweed and bitterweed (Helenium species), bitterweed (Hymenoxys odorata), rubberweed (Hymenoxys richardsonii), paperflowers (Psilostrophe species), groundsels (Senecio species), and cockleburs (Xanthium strumarium, X. spinosum). This list includes only those species that have caused the most problems. For details and information on other members of the Asteraceae that have caused livestock poisoning, refer to John Kingsbury’s Poisonous Plants of the United States and Canada (1964). FABACEAE—LEGUME FAMILY (FORMERLY LEGUMINOSAE) Many of our common foods, the beans and peas, come from the legume family. As we saw in Chapter 1, a number of wild native legumes also are edible. Quite a few legumes are toxic, though. In this section we’ll look at some plants that have caused fatalities in humans and some that have caused problems only for livestock. See “Fabaceae” in the index for the clues to recognizing the members of this family, one of the largest families of flowering plants in the world. In addition to the toxic plants mentioned below, other legumes have caused minor losses of cattle, horse, sheep, goats, swine, or poultry (Kingsbury 1964; Allen and Allen 1981; Ellis 1978; Lampe and Fagerström 1968; Turner 1959; Sperry et al. 1964; Hardin and Arena 1974). Wild indigos (Baptisia species) and partridge pea (Chamaecrista fasciculata) and its relatives the cassias and sennas contain toxins. Rattlepod (Crotalaria sagittalis) caused many deaths in livestock, particularly in horses, before the turn of the twentieth century. Two other members of the genus, Crotalaria spectabilis and C. retusa, were propagated as hay crops by the Bureau of Plant Industry in 1921, before it was discovered that the plants are highly toxic to livestock. A number of legumes provide valuable wild forage and cultivated fodder for livestock. Some forage plants cause serious illness or death, however, when they are allowed to serve as the major food source for the animals. Alfalfa, ordinarily an excellent source of fodder, can cause bloat in cattle and sheep if the diet is not supplemented with grasses. Mesquite pods provide a wonderful source of nutrition for cattle, but if they are not supplemented with other forage, the high sugar content in the pods disrupts digestion and can cause death. The lead trees (Leucaena species) contain a toxic alkaloid, mimosine, which causes hair loss if the forage comprises 10 percent or more of the animal’s diet (Ritchie 1979; Duke 1981). The lesson here seems to be that you can get too much of a good thing. Balanced with other forage, each of these plants provides good sources of protein and carbohydrates to livestock. Other fodders that seem to cause problems only when overeaten include the clovers (Trifolium) and vetches (Vicia). Guajillo (Acacia berlandieri), considered good wild forage in West Texas, can cause deaths if sheep and goats consume it as a major part of the diet over several months. Moldy hay from the sweet clovers (Melilotus species) and Korean bush clover (Kummerowia stipulacea, formerly classified as Lespedeza stipulacea) has caused death in cattle and other livestock. In sweet clover hay the mold creates coumarin, which breaks down into dicoumarin. Dicoumarin causes severe hemorrhaging. The discovery of coumarin in sweet clover mold led to the development of several important products, such as warfarin, which is used in rodent poisons, and an anticoagulant used medicinally to prevent and treat blood clots (Lewis and Elvin-Lewis 1977). Other wilted or moldy fodder crops, such as bird’s-foot trefoil (Lotus corniculatus), can form hydrocyanic acid, which is also highly toxic. Black Locust—Robinia pseudoacacia—Fabaceae (See photograph, plate 36.) The bark, leaves, sprouts, seeds, and pods of the black locust have poisoned horses, cattle, sheep, poultry, and humans. Small quantities of plant material can cause rapid and severe poisoning, but fatalities are rare. Horses have been poisoned by stripping the bark from a tree while tethered to it. Children have been poisoned from eating the seeds and from chewing the inner bark or sucking on the twigs (Kingsbury 1964; Lampe and Fagerström 1968; Ellis 1978; Watt and Breyer-Brandwijk 1962). Refer to the index for a description and further information on uses of the trees. The toxic compounds include toxalbumins, which are proteins that inhibit protein synthesis, thus producing severe toxicity. Bluebonnet, Lupine, Garbancillo—Lupinus Species—Fabaceae Several species of Lupinus have caused extensive loss of sheep in the western United States. As many as a thousand sheep have died at a time in Montana, Idaho, and Utah. The toxic species contain several deadly alkaloids. Sheep become ill and may die within one day if they graze an area heavy with the seeds, the most toxic part of the plants. Cattle and horses rarely become poisoned; they probably tend to avoid the seeds (Kingsbury 1964; Allen and Allen 1981; Stephens 1980; Schmutz and Hamilton 1979). Some western species that are thought to cause birth defects in livestock are: silky lupine (Lupinus sericeus, found from Arizona north to Canada), silvery lupine (L. argenteus, in Arizona and New Mexico north to Canada), broadleaf lupine (L. latifolius, from New Mexico to California and north to Washington), lunara lupine (L. formosus, only in California), tailcup lupine (L. caudatus, from the Texas Panhandle to California and north), velvet lupine (L. leucophyllus, from Northern California to Utah and north), and sulphur lupine (L. sulphureus, a yellow-flowered species only found in the Northwest). Rusty lupine (L. pusillus, found throughout the West, but not in Texas) may also be toxic. A number of species occurring in the Pacific Coast states may also be toxic (Forero, Nader, Craigmill, Ditomaso, Puschner, and Maas 2011). The toxic species tend to be high in the chemical anagyrine (Keeler 1988). But not all lupines are toxic. Several species of lupines provide good forage and serve as cover crops, restoring nitrogen to depleted soils. In some areas of the world, sweet varieties that are low in toxicity have been developed for human food, including several species found in the U.S.: narrowleaf lupine (Lupinus angustifolius), yellow lupine (L. luteus), and white lupine (L. albus), all three introduced as forage in Florida; sundial lupine (L. perennis, an eastern U.S. native that occurs in East Texas); and bigleaf lupine (L. polyphyllus, found in the Pacific Coast states and the Northwest) (Duke 1981; Summerfield and Bunting 1978). I have found no reports of livestock poisoning from the Texas species of Lupinus, except for L. caudatus. Apparently, cattle avoid Texas’s state flower, Lupinus texensis, and it may be a good cover crop (Turner 1959). PLANT CHARACTERISTICS The annual or perennial herbs sprout a basal rosette of palmately compound leaves in the winter. The showy spikes of blue to lavender or pink flowers emerge in the spring. Texas has seven species of Lupinus, all of them are called bluebonnets, and all of them are designated the state flower. There are 26 species in Arizona, and 18 species in New Mexico. Some species will have an occasional white population. A few species not found in our region have yellow flowers (Lupinus arboreus, a shrubby species in the Pacific Coast states; Lupinus arbustus, a species found in the western states from California and Utah north that can have purple or yellow flowers; and the northwestern species, L. sulphureus). Coral Bean, Colorín—Erythrina herbacea—Fabaceae (See illustration.) All parts of the coral bean shrub contain toxic alkaloids, but most poisonings occur when small children chew up the bright red seeds. Ingestion of one seed can cause serious illness. The toxic alkaloids have an action similar to that of curare, causing death from muscle paralysis. Erythrina crista-galli, a South American species sometimes planted as an ornamental in Texas, is one of the most toxic members of the genus (Allen and Allen 1981). The Mexican people use seeds of several species as a rat poison and the leaves and bark as a fish poison. Surprisingly, the flowers and leaves of some Mexican species are used for food (Turner 1959; Schmutz and Hamilton 1979; Watt and Breyer-Brandwijk 1962). Coral bean—Erythrina herbacea PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Erythrina herbacea is a thorny shrub that has compound leaves with three triangular or three-lobed leaflets. The long, slender bright red flowers, though papilionaceous, appear tubular. The dark pods are constricted between the seeds, which are scarlet. This is a southeastern U.S. species that grows in sandy woods in East Texas, particularly near the coast and inland to the Balcones Escarpment. Southwestern coral bean, also known as chilicote (Erythrina flabelliformis), is a shrub or tree, often with corky teeth on the trunk, that is native to the deserts of southern Arizona and New Mexico, and northern Mexico. Devil’s Shoestring, Catgut—Tephrosia virginiana—Fabaceae Native Americans have long used the root of this and several other species of Tephrosia as arrow poisons, fish poisons, insecticides, and even medicines (Ajilvsgi 1984). All parts of the plants are highly toxic. The insecticide rotenone comes from the leaves and seeds of an African species, Tephrosia vogelii. In restocking inland fisheries, only ten parts per million of the powdered seeds are needed to poison carp (Allen and Allen 1981; Duke 1981). PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Tephrosia virginiana is an eastern U.S. species that extends into Texas. Tephrosia species are not common, and one southwestern desert species, red hoarypea, Tephrosia vicioides, is rare. The low-growing perennial herbs usually have hairy foliage. The pinnately compound leaves may have few or many leaflets. The papilionaceous flowers may be reddish, purple, yellow, or creamy. Flattened and linear, the pods are 1–2 inches (3–6 cm) long. The common names are descriptive of the sinewy roots. Five species occur in Texas, three species occur in Arizona, and only Tephrosia vicioides occurs in New Mexico. Locoweed, Hierba Loca, Milk Vetch—Astragalus Species—Fabaceae Woolly locoweed—Astragalus mollissimus var. earlei (See illustration.) Locoweeds and other members of the genus Astragalus have caused extensive loss of cattle, horses, sheep, and goats in the western United States (Ajilvsgi 1984; Burlage 1968; Kingsbury 1964; Turner 1959; Warnock 1970; Forero, Nader, Craigmill, Ditomaso, Puschner, and Maas 2011). Decades of research into the problems associated with these plants have not fully defined the toxic principles of the genus. To add to the difficulties, not all species produce poisoning. In fact, some provide good forage for wildlife and domestic livestock. Because poisoning usually results from cumulative consumption of the plants, human poisoning is unlikely. The beans of one species, however, the ground plum (Astragalus crassicarpus), have a long history of use for human food (Gibbons and Tucker 1979; Allen and Allen 1981). Many Astragalus species occur in the United States. Because of the complexities of distinguishing one species from another, and the uncertainties about causes of poisoning, ranchers have difficulty determining whether the plants on their land are likely to cause problems. Poisoning has produced vastly different symptoms and has occurred under widely different sets of circumstances (Kingsbury 1964; Keeler, Van Kampen, and James 1978). Let’s look at the members of the genus Astragalus as three separate groups. ALKALOIDS The locoweeds contain an indolizidine alkaloid identified as swainsonine (Brown and Smith n.d.). Domestic animals, particularly horses, avidly seek out the plants, apparently becoming addicted to them. Horses, sheep, cattle, goats, and even elk have been poisoned by locoweeds. The toxin accumulates in the system of the animal, taking perhaps two months for symptoms of poisoning to appear. If the diet remains uncorrected after symptoms appear, the animals die in a few more weeks. The poison affects the central nervous system, and animals may become uncoordinated or excitable. They may tremble or become listless, unable to eat or drink. Extensive livestock loss has occurred from locoweed poisoning in the Southwest. Southwestern species known to cause this type of poisoning include: San Joaquin locoweed, A. asymmetricus (in California); sheep locoweed, A. nothoxys (in Arizona and New Mexico); Mt. Diablo milkvetch, A. oxyphysus (in California); Green River milkvetch, A. pubentissimus (in Utah, Wyoming, and Colorado); Thurber milkvetch, A. thurberi (in Arizona and New Mexico); freckled milkvetch, Astragalus lentiginosus (with many varieties throughout the western U.S.); Astragalus mollissimus (throughout much of the West), A. allochrous (throughout the Southwest), A. endopterus (in Arizona) and A. mollisimus var. earlei (in Texas and New Mexico). Oxytropis lambertii, a closely related species found throughout much of the West, also causes the same type of poisoning. SELENIUM ACCUMULATORS About two dozen species of Astragalus grow only in soils that contain selenium. These plants can accumulate enough selenium to cause death in livestock (Brown and Smith n.d.). A single large dose of highly toxic plant material or a cumulative amount of less toxic plants can cause poisoning. Fortunately, plants high in selenium are distasteful to livestock, and animals won’t usually eat them unless other forage is unavailable. Nonetheless, these plants also have caused extensive losses in cattle and sheep in the West. In New Mexico and Arizona, two-grooved milkvetch, Astragalus bisulcatus, accumulates selenium. Additional species known to develop this type of toxicity include: the whiteflowered A. pattersonii (in Arizona, New Mexico, Utah, and Colorado); stinking milkvetch, A. praelongus (found from New Mexico to Nevada); narrowleaf milkvetch (found north of New Mexico); and cream milkvetch, A. racemosus (in Texas and New Mexico, to the north). In Texas, only Astragalus racemosus is a known selenium accumulator. An interesting side benefit has developed from the discovery of plants that grow only in selenium soils. Scientists can use the plants to map areas high in selenium for the purpose of mining the valuable element. Some Astragalus species also provide good indicators of uranium ore and copper-molybdenum deposits. GLYCOSIDES Some locoweeds cause poisoning due to a nitropropanol-bearing glycoside identified as miserotoxin (Brown and Smith n.d.). This chemical gets its name from the species first found to contain this toxin, Astragalus miser, which occurs from Arizona and Nevada north to British Columbia. Poisoning causes respiratory distress and neurological malfunction. Other species that contain miserotoxin include: Astragalus convallarius, found in the western states north of Arizona; Russian milkvetch, A. falcatus, an introduced species grown north of our region; fourwing milkvetch, A. tetrapterus, native to Arizona and states to the north; and balloonpod milkvetch, A. whitneyi, found in the Pacific Coast states. Astragalus emoryanus (found from Texas to Arizona and Utah), an acceptable forage plant in some areas, seems to cause toxicity only in certain parts of its range, such as in the limestone of the Trans-Pecos, Texas, and the red sands along the Llano River, Texas. Relatively small quantities of plant material can be fatal. The yellow-flowered A. canadensis (which occurs through much of North America but not in Arizona) may also fit into this category (Keeler, Van Kampen, and James 1978). PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Astragalus species are low-growing perennial or annual herbs. They bear pinnately compound leaves, often with many tiny leaflets. The leaves may be basal or alternate on a leafy stem, but usually they form a clump close to the ground. The flower spikes emerge from the leaf axils. The papilionaceous flowers of most species are lilac to purple, though some species have greenishwhite or creamy-yellow blossoms. Thirty species grow in Texas, 80 species occur in Arizona, and 78 in New Mexico! Mescal Bean, Texas Mountain Laurel, Frijolillo—Sophora secundiflora— Fabaceae (See photograph, plate 39.) All parts of the mescal bean can poison livestock and humans (Sperry et al. 1964; Turner 1959; Allen and Allen 1981; Ellis 1978). A quarter pound of foliage can kill a hundred-pound calf. One well-chewed seed may be enough to kill a person (Schmutz and Hamilton 1979; Kingsbury 1964; Izaddoost, Harris, and Gracy 1976). Fortunately, the highly toxic seeds have a hard seed coat. The seed can go through the digestive tract intact and cause no harm, which probably explains why human poisonings are very rare (Lampe and Fagerström 1968). Children sometimes use the hard red seeds in games. Children refer to them as hot beans because if you rub the bean rapidly on the sidewalk, it becomes hot to the touch. Though Warnock (1970) reports that the aroma of the flowers can cause nausea, the Texas State Poison Center has no reports of such problems. In addition to the seeds, the leaves and flowers are toxic (Kingsbury 1964; Sperry, Dollahite, Hoffman, and Camp 1964). Native Americans in the Southwest used powdered mescal bean seeds as a hallucinogenic drug (Ricciuti 1978; Emboden 1979; Vines 1960). Archeologists digging in rock shelters in the Trans-Pecos, Texas (Adovasio and Fry 1976), have determined that groups living in the shelters used the seeds from at least 8400 B.C. until after A.D. 1000. The seeds may still be in use in ceremonies of the Plains tribes, though perhaps only as decoration. Peyote gradually has replaced mescal bean as a hallucinogen, probably because peyote is much less dangerous to use. PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Mescal bean forms an attractive shrub and often is used as an ornamental in Central Texas. The evergreen leaves and the beautiful spring flowers give it year-round appeal. The pinnately compound leaves have smooth shiny leaflets. Hanging in large showy clusters, the blue to purple flowers have a strong aroma very much like that of grape Kool-Aid. The dark-colored woody pod is constricted between the large seeds. This species is native to Texas, New Mexico, and northern Mexico. The flowers, foliage, and leathery black pods of the related Eve’s necklace (Styphnolobium affine, formerly classified as Sophora affinis) probably also are poisonous (Turner 1959). The long pods of Eve’s necklace are so deeply constricted between the seeds that they resemble a string of black pop beads (refer to the index for further description). Eve’s necklace has white to pale violet flowers and is native to Texas, Louisiana, Oklahoma, and Arkansas. Rattlebush, Bagpod, Bequilla—Sesbania Species—Fabaceae (See illustration, page 253, and photograph, plate 35.) All four southwestern species of Sesbania have poisoned livestock. The seeds contain the highest level of toxic saponins, but the foliage and flowers also are toxic. At least one child has died from eating the seeds of the rattlebush, Sesbania drummondii, native from the southeastern U.S. into Texas. Deaths in cattle, goats, sheep, and fowl have occurred from single doses or from cumulative small doses of the toxic seeds. As few as nine seeds of Brazil rattlebush (S. punicea, introduced into the southeastern U.S., and occurring in Texas) will kill a hen, and a couple of ounces of seeds of either S. drummondii or bagpod (S. vesicaria, native from the southeastern U.S. into Texas) can kill a sheep (Kingsbury 1964; Vines 1960; Sperry et al. 1964). The Yumas used the stem fibers of bequilla, Sesbania herbacea (see photograph, plate 35; native to the southern half of the U.S., but not New Mexico), as cordage for making fishing nets. Extract the fibers from the bark of the long, slender stems by soaking them in water for a few days. A fine paper, similar to rice paper, can be made from the soft pith of bequilla. This species is planted in citrus orchards in the United States for soil restoration (Schmutz and Hamilton 1979; Allen and Allen 1981; Duke 1981; Schery 1972). PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Bequilla and bagpod are tall annuals, and the two rattlebushes are shrubs. All four species have large pinnately compound leaves with numerous leaflets. The papilionaceous flowers are yellow or red. The most distinctive characteristics are the pods. Bagpod has two seeds in a smooth papery pod that is about 3 inches (8 cm) long and 3/4 inch (2 cm) wide. The pod is inflated and splits open when dry. Bequilla has a smooth, many-seeded linear pod 8–12 inches (2–3 dm) long and only about 1/8 inch (4 mm) wide. The rattlebushes bear dry, somewhat woody pods with four winged edges. About 3 inches (8 cm) long and 1/2 inch (12 mm) wide, the pods hold several seeds that rattle inside when the pod is dry. Rattlebush—Sesbania drummondii Singletary Pea—Lathyrus hirsutus, Lathyrus pusillus, and Other Species— Fabaceae Sweetpea—Lathyrus latifolius, Lathyrus odoratus—Fabaceae Members of the genus Lathyrus have caused many cases of illness and death in humans and livestock over the centuries in Europe, Russia, India, and northern Africa. Livestock become poisoned when the seeds mature on forage vines and become a significant part of the animal’s diet. Human poisoning from L. sativus, rarely found wild in the United States, has occurred during times of drought and famine when people have relied on the seeds as a major food source (Allen and Allen 1981; Kingsbury 1964; Lewis and Elvin-Lewis 1977). The sweetpeas and singletary peas are Old World vines cultivated in the United States and occasionally found growing wild in the Southwest. Farmers grow singletary peas as cover crops and occasionally as winter forage. Livestock poisoning from the forages led to experiments with other species, including the ornamental sweetpea Lathyrus odoratus and the perennial sweetpea Lathyrus latifolius. Lathyrus latifolius was found to be the most highly toxic, causing convulsions and death. With the forage species and L. odoratus, symptoms appear when the seeds become a major part of the animal’s diet, typically greater than 25 percent for a period of days or weeks. The seeds cause paralysis, skeletal deformity, birth defects, and, if the diet remains unaltered, death. PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Lathyrus species typically are vining herbs. The pinnately compound leaves are tipped with a tendril. The showy papilionaceous flowers may be white, pink, red, or bluish. The linear to oblong pods have thin walls. Wisteria—Wisteria Species—Fabaceae (See photograph, plate 10.) The native and the introduced ornamental vines have toxic seeds, pods, and bark. I have found no human deaths reported from the plants in the United States. Nonetheless, ingestion of even one or two seeds has caused severe poisoning in children. A glycoside named wisterine and a lectin are thought to be the toxic chemicals. Violent vomiting, diarrhea, and gastrointestinal pain come on quickly, but with immediate medical treatment, recovery is rapid (Kingsbury 1964; Ellis 1978; Lampe and Fagerström 1968). Though some people maintain that the flowers are edible, the Texas State Poison Center reports cases of violent vomiting due to the ingestion of the flowers. Dogs and cats also are poisoned by wisteria. PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Wisterias form thick high-climbing vines and have large pinnately compound leaves with leaflets that may be 2-1/2 inches (6.5 cm) long. Fragrant purple papilionaceous flowers hang in long, loose racemes. The straight, rather flat pods are several inches long, and those of the Chinese wisteria (Wisteria sinensis), widely cultivated throughout the southeastern U.S. into Texas, are covered with velvety hairs. The native species, Wisteria frutescens, occurring throughout the eastern states into East Texas, has smooth pods and is not common. Japanese wisteria, W. floribunda, also occurs throughout the southeast. GELSEMIACEAE—JESSAMINE FAMILY Jasmine, Carolina Jasmine, Yellow Jessamine, Jamín Amarillo—Gelsemium sempervirens—Gelsemiaceae (See illustration.) This high-climbing or trailing vine has caused severe poisoning in humans. All parts contain toxic alkaloids. A child nearly died from eating the flowers (Michael Ellis, interview, January 1986). The toxic nectar may produce poisonous honey. Numerous adult poisonings occurred early in the twentieth century when the root was used as a medicinal herb to treat migraine headaches and neuralgia. The roots contain a wide range of chemical compounds. Two of the toxic compounds are gelsemine and gelseminine, which are motor nerve depressants (Demers 1998). Familiarize your children with the hazards of Carolina jasmine since the vine is often used as an ornamental (Hardin and Arena 1974; Kingsbury 1964; Lampe and Fagerström 1968). PLANT CHARACTERISTICS The smooth slender vine grows in open woods on sandy soil throughout the southeastern states into East Texas and along the Texas coast, and into Mexico. The simple semievergreen leaves occur in pairs. They are lanceolate to ovate and about 1–3 inches (2.5–7.5 cm) long and half as wide, with entire margins. The petals unite to form a funnel-shaped blossom with five lobes. The fragrant yellow flowers are radially symmetrical and about 1–1-1/2 inches (2.5–3.5 cm) long. Carolina jasmine—Gelsemium sempervirens Death camas—Zigadenus nuttallii MELANTHIACEAE—FALSE-HELLEBORE FAMILY (CLASSIFIED BY SOME AS PART OF THE LILIACEAE) Death Camas—Zigadenus Species The plants known as death camas (Zigadenus species, illustrated) contain highly toxic alkaloids. All parts of the plants are toxic (Kingsbury 1964; Weddle 1980). Several western species have caused extensive loss of life in livestock, particularly sheep. Humans have died from eating the bulbs, which can easily be mistaken for edible plants, particularly the very similar Camassia species (refer to the index). Death camas flowers also have caused severe poisoning in children. The flowers may be white, yellow, or greenish. The basal leaves are grasslike, and the stem leaves are reduced to bracts. Nine species grow in the Southwest. PHYTOLACCACEAE—POKEWEED FAMILY Coralito, Small Pokeweed, Rouge Plant—Rivina humilis (See photograph, plate 53.) All parts of coralito are considered toxic, with toxicity similar to that of the larger pokeweed, described below (Lampe and Fagerström 1968). You can use the red berries of this low-growing perennial herb as an ink and a dye (see Chapter 7). Refer to the index for more information. Poke, Pokeweed, Ñamoli—Phytolacca americana—Phytolaccaceae (See illustration.) Poke—Phytolacca americana For those people who are aware of the pitfalls of its misuse, the poke can provide a tasty potherb. The berries yield delightful dyes for wool (see Chapter 7), and in the past, poke has had value in folk medicine. But misused, poke is a deadly poisonous plant. The toxic compounds, including an alkaloid called phytolaccatoxin, concentrate in the bark, root, green berries, and older leaves of poke. All plant parts contain some degree of toxicity, however, including the uncooked shoots and the tempting purple berries. Though the ripe berries reportedly have been cooked in pies, a few raw berries can cause severe poisoning or death in infants. Consider the cooked berries potentially toxic also. Poke is emetic, purgative, and narcotic. Poisoning may cause severe cramps, vomiting, and diarrhea. Convulsions can also develop. With proper treatment, though, recovery usually is rapid, and fatalities rare (Ellis 1978; Hardin and Arena 1974; Kingsbury 1964; Lampe and Fagerström 1968; Watt and BreyerBrandwijk 1962). In some cases of poisonings from the berries, pathologists have noted changes in the chemistry of the blood cells of the victims (Barker, Farnes, and LaMarche 1966). The blood changes have had no apparent harmful effects, and cooking apparently inactivates the chemical involved. Nevertheless, as this mitogenic property (the substance affecting blood cells) can be absorbed through open wounds in the skin, it is advisable to wear gloves when handling the berries and roots, the parts of the plant that contain the mitogens (Lewis and Elvin-Lewis 1977). The young spring shoots are gathered wild for food throughout the eastern United States and are cultivated in Europe and northern Africa. As most people who grew up using the greens know, if the plant is collected at the wrong stage or if it is eaten raw, it can cause poisoning. But poke greens furnish a fine vegetable for those who can positively identify the young spring growth. Gather only the very young shoots, less than about 8 inches (20 cm) tall, taking care not to collect any of the highly toxic roots. Don’t let the common name “poke salet” confuse you. The term “salet” does not have the same meaning as “salad.” Poke salet is a potherb, not a raw vegetable. Cook the greens thoroughly, changing the water once, to extract the toxic compounds. First, boil the shoots in water for about 10 minutes. Then pour off the water; throw it out. Re-cook the greens in a small amount of water, adding seasonings such as onions and bacon fat. Cook the greens slowly for about 30 minutes, and serve them with cornbread and pepper sauce (Abbott 1982; Arnott 1975; Peterson 1977). The Cherokees and other Native American tribes and early European settlers used poke roots to treat a variety of ailments. From 1820 to 1916 The United States Pharmacopeia listed poke as a medicinal, as did the National Formulary until 1947. The roots are highly toxic, though, and the herbal remedies occasionally have caused poisoning. Research indicates a number of potentially valuable uses for poke in modern medicine. The mitogens present in the berries and root may prove useful for studying immune responses and diagnosing certain diseases. Microbiologists at Southwest Texas State University have investigated a protein present in the leaves that may provide an antiviral agent for treating cold sores and influenza. Leaf extracts also may have antibacterial effects. As it has in the past, poke seems destined to continue as a valued medicinal in the future (Kinghorn 1979; B. E. Barker, letter to the author, December 27, 1985). Research continues on the potential in treating cancers, HIV, influenza, skin disorders, and other diseases (Yang, Wieczorck, Allen, and Nett 2003). Extracts of a tropical species are also being proposed for use in preventing the spread of zebra mussels (Treyvaud, Marston, Dyatmiko, and Hostettmann 2000). The crimson juice of the purple berries yields a pink to red dyebath, or “lake.” Though the dye fades considerably with exposure to light, it is one of the few bright red dyes from plants. You can even obtain blue to black dyes from pokeberries. During the Civil War, people used the juice as ink. The Kiowas used the juice as a red stain for decoration and dried the berries to use in necklaces (Vestal and Schultes 1939). Fernald, Kinsey, and Rollins (1958) report that in the 1780s the Portuguese intensified the color of poorly made red wines by adulterating them with pokeberry juice. PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Poke, a perennial herb, sends up a woody much-branched stalk 4–9 feet (1–3 m) tall. The smooth bark is bright red to purple. The simple alternate leaves may be 5–10 inches (1–2.5 dm) in length. The smooth leaves typically are broadly lanceolate and have entire margins. White flowers appear in a large raceme from summer to fall, and the drooping berry clusters form in late summer and fall. The stalk that holds the berries is often bright red. The berries, about 1/3 inch (8–10 mm) in diameter, turn dark purple when ripe, and the juice leaves a red stain. Poke grows in rich low ground, particularly in disturbed open areas throughout the eastern United States and southeastern Canada. It extends across the southern U.S. from coast to coast, and up the Pacific Coast to Washington. Two additional species occurs in the Southwest, red inkplant, Phytolacca octandra, a worldwide species sometimes found in Arizona, and tropical pokeweed, Phytolacca icosandra, which occurs from Arizona to South America. ROSACEAE—ROSE FAMILY See information below, under “Poisons in the Garden and the Kitchen,” for the toxic plant parts of many edible fruits that belong to this family. A few members of the family cause poisoning in livestock. Mountain mahogany, Cercocarpus montanus, a mountain shrub that is scattered throughout the West, generally considered desirable forage for livestock and wild game, can cause copper toxicity in livestock, if they have limited supplemental forage. The roots are emetic (cause vomiting) to humans and have had value in traditional medicine and ceremonies, particularly among the Navajos. SAPINDACEAE—SOAPBERRY FAMILY Buckeye, Horse-chestnut—Aesculus Species (See illustration.) The seeds of Aesculus species resemble a chestnut, but the buckeyes and horsechestnuts are not related to the true chestnut. Aesculus seeds are deadly poisonous. (The true chestnut tree, Castanea dentata, bears an edible nut, but an infestation of chestnut blight virtually exterminated the chestnut tree in North America.) The exact nature of the toxins in Aesculus remains undetermined but may include the saponin glycoside aesculin. All parts of the shrubs and trees can cause poisoning (Hardin and Arena 1974; Ellis 1978; Lampe and Fagerström 1968; Kingsbury 1964). A few fatalities in children are on record. Children have been poisoned from eating the seeds and the green capsules and from drinking a tea of the leaves of the European horsechestnut (Aesculus hippocastanum), a tree planted as an ornamental in the northeastern United States. All of the species found in Texas have caused illness in livestock. Cattle have become ill from grazing on seedlings and sprouts. Even honey produced from the nectar of the flowers may be poisonous to humans. Fernald, Kinsey, and Rollins (1958) report that Native Americans poisoned fish by throwing the powdered seeds and branches into ponds. The stunned fish floated to the surface, making them easy to catch. Lewis and Elvin-Lewis (1977) state that the European horse-chestnut contains an agent that may be useful in medicine for treating shock. The roots of Aesculus pavia have been used for making soap, and Vines (1960) reports that the compound aesculin in ointment form can be used to treat sunburn. Researchers at the National Center for Pharmaceutical Crops at Stephen F. Austin University in Texas have isolated 31 chemical compounds from seeds of red buckeye, Aesculus pavia. Several of these compounds have potential value in treating cancer (Zhang and Li 2007). Red buckeye—Aesculus pavia var. pavia PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Buckeyes and horse-chestnuts are shrubs or trees with large palmately compound leaves that occur opposite each other on the branches. The deciduous leaves of some species fall from the shrub by midsummer. The margins of the large leaflets are serrated. The beautiful showy flowers, which are bilateral, fivelobed, and bell-shaped, form large erect inflorescences that bloom in spring. A leathery tan capsule, the fruit may be smooth or spiny. The capsule holds one to three shiny brown seeds, each about 1–11/2 inch (2.5–4 cm) in diameter with a pale spot at one end. In Texas, buckeyes grow along stream banks and river bottoms, in woods, and on rocky slopes. Our native species make attractive ornamentals. The scarletflowered shrub Aesculus pavia is found throughout the southeastern U.S., into East Texas to the edge of the Edwards Plateau, while a yellow-flowered variety occurs on the Edwards Plateau and near Houston, Texas. Ohio buckeye (Aesculus glabra), a shrub to small tree with pale yellow or yellowish-green flowers, grows throughout much of the northeastern U.S. to northeast and Central Texas. These two species hybridize, resulting in flowers with color combinations of yellow and red. There are no species of Aesculus in Arizona and New Mexico. Mexican Buckeye, Monilla—Ungnadia speciosa—Sapindaceae (See photograph, plate 59.) The seeds of Mexican buckeye contain cyanogenetic lipids, a highly toxic cyanide bound up in lipid form (Seigler, Seaman, and Mabry 1971; Selmar, Grocholewski, and Seigler 1990). Toxicity may vary considerably, as some individuals have eaten the seeds with no ill effects (Scooter Cheatham, interview, March 1985). A single seed gave me mild stomach discomfort, warning me not to eat any more. David Seigler (letter to the author, March 31, 1986) warns that five or six seeds could be deadly. The leaves are also toxic. Native Americans may have used the seeds as a fish poison and an arrow poison. Archeologists surveying rock shelters in West Texas discovered Mexican buckeye seeds frequently stored with mescal bean seeds (Sophora secundiflora, see page 251). Southwestern tribes have used the highly toxic mescal beans as a hallucinogen for centuries. That the beans were stored together indicates that they may also have used Mexican buckeye seeds as a hallucinogen. Chemical analysis of the seeds, however, indicates that they do not contain any psychoactive chemicals (Adovasio and Fry 1976). PLANT CHARACTERISTICS While in the same family, Mexican buckeye is not the same as the true buckeye (see “buckeye” above). Ungnadia speciosa is a shrub or small tree with pinnately compound leaves that alternate on the branches. The large leaflets are serrated. Fragrant rose-colored flowers appear in the spring. The tree is most readily recognized by its pods. Often reddish, the three-lobed woody pod holds three round seeds. The hard shiny seeds, about 1/2 inch (1.5 cm) in diameter, are dark brown to black with a pale scar. The pods cling to the branches long after the seeds have dropped to the ground. Mexican buckeye grows in the western two-thirds of Texas to Dallas County, and in southern New Mexico and Mexico. The tree is found in wooded canyons and on rocky hillsides. SOLANACEAE—NIGHTSHADE FAMILY The name “nightshade” brings to mind poisonous plants such as the deadly nightshade (Atropa bella-donna), a European plant used as a source of the medicinal drug atropine. In spite of its bad reputation, the nightshade family includes many important garden vegetables. Tomatoes, potatoes, eggplant, bell pepper, and jalapeños: all are nightshades. “Solanum” probably comes from the Latin “solamen” (meaning “quieting”), referring to the sedative properties of some species. Perhaps the most infamous family member in that regard is tobacco. Most of these agricultural crops come from the New World, with eggplant originating in tropical Asia. When the tomato was first imported to Europe, people were aghast to think of eating a nightshade. Nonetheless, Europe and the rest of the world soon gained a taste for these curious fruits and their relatives. Even the edible nightshades have their darker side, though, and can cause poisoning. Take a look at the “Poisons in the Garden” section in this chapter for a discussion of the poisonous potential of tomatoes, potatoes, and their relatives. Note that if you are allergic to one member of this large family, you may develop an allergy to all or many other members. The highly diverse genus Solanum comprises more than a thousand species worldwide, including the potato and the eggplant. Solanine is the predominant glyco-alkaloid, though others may also be present. Solanine is highly toxic and can cause death, but the degree of toxicity varies from species to species and within a species. A single species, such as the black nightshade (Solanum nigrum or S. americanum) may be highly toxic in one area of the world and yet considered edible in another area (Hackett and Carolane 1982). Black nightshade is described in Chapter 4. Three southwestern Solanum species known as wild potatoes allegedly have edible but bitter tubers: Solanum jamesii, S. leptosepalum, and S. fendleri (Gibbons and Tucker 1979). All three are uncommon to rare species, so whether or not the tubers are safe for food, they are not available. You can consider all species of Solanum to have toxic foliage and berries. Besides silverleaf nightshade and Jerusalem-cherry, described in more detail below, the foliage and fruit (ripe and unripe) of the following species are known to have caused human or livestock poisoning: black nightshade (Solanum americanum, found across the southern third of the U.S. and along the Pacific Coast; and Solanum nigrum, not found in our region), buffalo bur (Solanum rostratum, found throughout most of North America; see photograph, plate 61), horse nettles (Solanum carolinense, in much of North America, and Solanum dimidiatum, in much of the southern U.S., except Arizona), cutleaf nightshade (Solanum triflorum, in much of North America), cockroach berry (Solanum capsicoides, introduced into the southeastern U.S., and into East Texas), and hairy nightshade (Solanum villosum, introduced into Florida and California). A very few wild genera of Solanaceae may be used for food. The ripe fruit of the ground-cherry (Physalis) is edible, and the ripe fruit of the chile pequín (Capsicum annuum) furnishes a peppery spice. Refer to the index for information and cautions on using these two nightshades. Meanwhile, you will do well to assume that the fruit of all other members of the family can cause poisoning, and that the foliage of all species, including those with edible fruit, is toxic. In addition to references cited below, I relied on Kingsbury (1964), Hardin and Arena (1974), Lampe and Fagerström (1968), Ellis (1978), Heiser (1969), and Keeler, Van Kampen, and James (1978) for information on toxic members of Solanaceae. Family Characteristics Members of the Solanaceae family grow as herbs, vines, shrubs, or small trees. The best characteristics to assist you in recognizing the family are those of the flowers. Examine a petunia or a tomato blossom as an example. The flowers vary considerably in size, from the half-inch blooms of Jerusalem-cherry to the six-inch flowers of jimsonweed, but they all have five petals joined together to form either an open star, a bell, a tube, or a trumpet. The flowers are radially symmetrical, and the stamens (usually five) may merge together to form a prominent yellow beak. The fruit may be a succulent berry or a dry capsule. The leaves are often simple and usually alternate on the stems. Jerusalem-cherry—Solanum pseudocapsicum—Solanaceae The fruit and leaves of Jerusalem-cherry are highly poisonous. A shrubby plant, it is used as an ornamental indoors and outdoors and may be found growing wild in disturbed soils and woodlands in the southeastern U.S., into Southeast Texas. The plant may be confused with the chile pequín (Capsicum annuum). The yellow to red succulent berry of the Jerusalem-cherry is round. The narrow or oblong leaves, up to 4 inches (1 dm) long, have entire or slightly wavy margins. The fruit of varieties of chile pequín may be nearly round but are usually oval, or long and tapering. For further descriptions of both plants, refer to the index. Jessamine, Jasmine—Cestrum Species—Solanaceae Three ornamental Cestrum shrubs occasionally may be found wild in South Texas and along the Texas coast. All parts are toxic to humans, livestock, and pets. The succulent berries attract small children and have caused severe poisoning. In the greenish unripe fruits, the alkaloid solanine predominates while the ripe berries are high in tropane-related alkaloids. The variable alkaloid content makes symptoms unpredictable. The poisons can cause severe gastrointestinal distress, hallucinations, paralysis, and death. PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Jessamines are evergreen or deciduous shrubs. The simple leaves are alternate, the margins entire. The oblong or lanceolate blades may be 2–6 inches (5–15 cm) long. The small, fragrant flowers form a slender tube or trumpet about 1 inch (2.5 cm) long. Round or oval, the berries are about 3/8 inch (1 cm) long. The day-blooming jessamine, Cestrum diurnum, introduced into Texas and Florida, has white flowers and black berries. The night-blooming jessamine, Cestrum nocturnum, introduced into the southeastern states into Texas and also in California, has greenish-white to yellowish flowers, particularly fragrant at night, and white berries. Willow-leaved jessamine, Cestrum parqui, introduced into Texas and California, has yellowish-green flowers, also especially fragrant at night, and purplish-brown berries. Jimsonweed, Toloache—Datura Species—Solanaceae (See illustration.) In 1676, during the colonial rebellion in Jamestown, Virginia, Royalist soldiers boiled a pot of greens gathered from the wild. The greens caused the men to hallucinate and become partially crazed for several days. The plant the soldiers consumed, Datura stramonium, still carries the name jamestown weed, or jimsonweed, in memory of the event (Beverley 1947). Various species of Datura have been put to use as hallucinogens by the Algonquins in their manhood initiation rites and by the Hopis, the Zunis, and several other Native American tribes, sometimes resulting in the death of the initiates (Emboden 1979). In India, criminals called dhataureas used the drug in knockout drops. All parts of jimsonweed are highly toxic, with the seeds, leaves, and roots containing high concentrations of the alkaloids atropine, hyoscyamine, and scopolamine. Atropine, for example, is an anticholinergic agent, which affects the central nervous system. During both world wars, when doctors in the United States faced a shortage of atropine, jimsonweed was cultivated as a substitute source. Jimsonweed—Datura wrightii According to the 1981 report of the National Clearinghouse for Poison Control Centers, 89 percent of cases of jimsonweed ingestion resulted in poisoning. Adult poisonings usually occur from attempts to use the plant to induce hallucinations. Small children are accidentally poisoned from eating the seeds, chewing the leaves, or sucking the nectar from the flowers. Adults usually recover from the toxic effects, but a few grams (a fraction of an ounce) of the leaf or seeds can kill a child. Livestock and pets also succumb to this highly toxic plant. Even honey made from the poisonous nectar is considered dangerous. In 2010, one of the two human fatalities from plants was from a close relative, angel’s tears, Brugmansia suaveolens, in Florida (Bronstein, Spyker, Cantilena Jr., Green, Rumack, and Dart 2011). PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Datura species are annual or perennial herbs with lush bad-smelling foliage. The fragrant trumpet-shaped flowers, 3–6 inches (7–15 cm) long, are white to violet. The fruit is a round, often spiny capsule about 1–2 inches (3–5 cm) in diameter, with numerous large seeds. Datura stramonium, an introduced species that grows wild throughout much of North America, is occasionally used as an ornamental in our region. Sacred thorn-apple, Datura wrightii, a native species, is found in much of the U.S. Four species occur in Texas, five occur in Arizona, and four in New Mexico. Nightshade, Silverleaf Nightshade, Trompillo—Solanum elaeagnifolium— Solanaceae (See illustration.) Trompillo is a noxious weed in crops, particularly cotton. In Texas the plant has caused considerable loss of cattle, which are highly susceptible to poisoning from the fruit. Unlike the fruit of some other members of the genus, the ripe berries are more toxic than the unripe fruit. A few ounces can cause severe poisoning or death in livestock and humans, though human fatalities are rare. Silverleaf nightshade, trompillo—Solanum elaeagnifolium In spite of its toxicity, the plant has been put to a variety of practical uses. The Pimas used the berry as a substitute for rennet in making cheese. The Kiowas combined the fruit with brain tissue for tanning hides (Vestal and Schultes 1939). Silverleaf nightshade contains solasodine, a chemical used in the manufacture of steroids (Boyd, Murray, and Tyrl 1984; Hodgson 2001). The unripe fruit or the whole plant can be used to dye wool. PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Trompillo is a spiny (occasionally spineless) perennial herb 2–3 feet (6–9 dm) tall. The stems and bottoms of the leaves have a coating of silvery hairs. The oblong simple leaves, 2–6 inches (5–15 cm) long, have entire to wavy margins. Appearing from spring to fall, the purple (occasionally white) blossoms are about 1 inch (2.5 cm) in diameter. The flowers are star-shaped with the yellow stamens forming a protruding beak. About 5/8 inch (15 mm) in diameter, the round, hard berry resembles a tiny tomato. The unripe berry is green, turning yellow, then black in summer and fall. Trompillo grows throughout the southern half of the U.S. and into Mexico. It is particularly abundant in dry limestone and disturbed soils. Tobacco—Nicotiana tabacum—Solanaceae Tree Tobacco, Desert Tobacco—Nicotiana Species—Solanaceae (See photograph, plate 62.) The members of the genus Nicotiana contain the deadly poisonous alkaloid nicotine and several other alkaloids. The tobacco used in cigarettes is Nicotiana tabacum, which is found only in cultivation. Its native origins are uncertain. Ingestion of cultivated tobacco leaves has caused numerous human and livestock deaths. Children have died from swallowing small amounts of tobacco. People have also died from eating the raw or cooked leaves of the wild tree tobacco (Nicotiana glauca) and the desert tobacco (Nicotiana obtusifolia). The flower nectar is also toxic. For further information on tobacco poisoning, see the index. PLANT CHARACTERISTICS Tree tobacco, Nicotiana glauca, is a tall, slender introduced shrub with alternate simple leaves that have entire margins. A waxy white coating covers the smooth ovate leaves. The tubular yellow flowers are about 1–2 inches (2.5–4.5 cm) long. The plant grows across much of the southern third of the U.S. Desert tobacco, Nicotiana obtusifolia, is a biennial or perennial native herb up to 3 feet (1 m) tall. The oblong leaves are covered with sticky hairs, and the tubular flowers, about 1 inch (2.5 cm) long, are greenish-white or yellowish. It grows from South and West Texas to California, north to Nevada, and into Mexico. Four species of Nicotiana are native to Arizona, two to New Mexico, and three are native to Texas. TOXIC ORNAMENTAL HOUSEPLANTS AND YARD PLANTS: THE ENEMY WITHIN In 1982, approximately two-thirds of the plant ingestions reported to the National Clearinghouse for Poison Control Centers (NCPCC) involved ornamentals, and more than 80 percent of the victims were children under five years old (Mark Fow, letter and documents to the author, November 21, 1984). That trend continues in the most recent annual report of the American Association of Poison Control Centers (2010). These statistics emphasize the importance of learning the identity and toxic potential of the plants in and around your own home. Many plants that poison humans can also poison small pets, such as canaries, parakeets, cats, and dogs. Michael Ellis (interview, January 1986) notes that 10 percent of all poisoning cases reported to the Texas State Poison Center involve pets. While human deaths from wild and ornamental plants are extremely rare, the death of a pet is more likely. Every year a number of cats and dogs die from chewing the leaves of houseplants, such as schefflera and philodendron, and birds are poisoned from toxic seeds. The small body size of children and pets makes them more susceptible to toxins than adults. In addition, the delicate metabolism of cats makes them vulnerable to poisoning by plants that are harmless to humans and other animals. Oleander—Nerium oleander Castor bean—Ricinus communis For the safety of your family and pets, review this list and note those plants that grow in your home environment. The list includes ornamentals commonly used in landscapes and as houseplants. These plants can cause moderate to serious illness. A number of them are considered highly dangerous and have caused numerous deaths in humans, pets, and livestock. Toxic symptoms may begin immediately or be delayed several hours or even days. If anyone, including your pets, consumes any quantity of the plants listed here or any plant of which you are unsure, you should contact your doctor, veterinarian, or Poison Control Center. Start now to teach your children about the possible hazards of eating ornamental and wild plants. Refer to page 226 for recommended first aid procedures and further information on plant poisoning. Other plants native to the Southwest that are used in landscaping may be listed below. If you have a plant not found on this list or elsewhere in this book, do not assume that it is safe. For many plants, reliable information on toxicity simply is not available. Some ornamentals that have been considered nontoxic in previous publications may in fact be toxic. The 1982 NCPCC report shows cases of poisoning occurring with some plants that other regional Poison Control Center publications listed as nontoxic. Studies on rats and mice, conducted by Der Marderosian and Roia, indicate that a number of plants previously considered to be nontoxic can cause poisoning (Kinghorn 1979). The list below is alphabetical by common name (rather than family name), to make it easier to find ornamental plants. For details on the toxicity of wild plants in your environment, refer to the index. Some of these are discussed in more detail, either earlier in this chapter (listed by scientific family name) or in Chapter 4, in the section on toxic fruits. Also refer to the index for possible additional information on plants listed here. In addition to references cited below, I relied on the following for information on toxic ornamentals: Ellis 1978; Kinghorn 1979; Kingsbury 1964, 1965; Hardin and Arena 1974; Lampe and Fagerström 1968; Keeler, Van Kampen, and James 1978; Peterson 1977; James 1973; Mitchell and Rook 1979; Ricciuti 1978; Schmutz and Hamilton 1979; Larkin 1983; National Clearinghouse for Poison Control Centers 1981 bulletin and documents for 1982 (Mark Fow, letter and documents to the author, November 21, 1984); Michael Ellis, interview, January 1986; Bronstein, Spyker, Cantilena Jr., Green, Rumack, and Dart 2011. AMARYLLIS FAMILY—AMARYLLIDACEAE Many members of the family contain toxic alkaloids, including the following garden flowers: Amaryllis, Crinum, daffodil, jonquil, and narcissus (all three Narcissus species), spider lily (Hymenocallis), and atamasco lily (Zephyranthes atamasca). Though all parts may potentially cause poisoning, the bulbs are the most toxic part. Human poisoning rarely occurs unless the bulbs are mistaken for onions or other edible bulbs. I have found no reports of human deaths from these plants, but pets and livestock have died from consuming them. The bulbs can cause an allergic dermatitis in sensitive individuals. For further information on toxic wild members of the family, refer to the index, and information earlier in this chapter. ARUM FAMILY—ARACEAE Caladium—Caladium Species Calla—Calla palustris, Zantedeschia aethiopica Dumbcane—Dieffenbachia Species Elephant Ear—Colocasia esculenta Giant Elephant Ear—Alocasia Species Philodendron—Philodendron Species, Monstera Species Pothos, Devil’s Ivy—Epipremnum aureum and Other Species Several common houseplants belong to the arum family. Chewing on the leaves, stems, or roots of these plants can cause burning and swelling of the mouth, tongue, and throat. Needlelike calcium oxalate crystals and histamine release are two possible causes of the reaction. The swelling can inhibit speech for several days, accounting for the common name “dumbcane” for the Dieffenbachia species. Gastrointestinal distress may also occur. The sap of the plants can irritate the skin and cause eye damage. The dumbcanes and the philodendrons typically account for more than half of all plant poisonings handled by the Texas State Poison Center (Michael Ellis, interview, January 1986). In 2010, among the plants most commonly reported to poison control centers nationwide were peace lilies (Spathiphyllum species, also called spathe flowers) and philodendrons (Philodendron and Monstera species). Because the plants burn the mouth, children rarely swallow them, and so human poisoning is usually moderate. Cats and dogs have died, however, from chewing on a number of these species. Some have the potential to cause death in humans, both from the toxic compounds in them and from suffocation, in the event of severe swelling of the tongue and throat. In addition, some individuals develop an allergic dermatitis reaction to these plants. Refer to the index for information on toxic wild members of the family. AZALEA, RHODODENDRON—RHODODENDRON SPECIES— ERICACEAE All parts of these ornamental shrubs can cause poisoning. They contain grayanotoxins, which can cause paralysis, convulsions, and death in humans. Children have been poisoned from eating the flowers or making a tea of the leaves. Even honey from the nectar can be toxic. BIRD OF PARADISE, POINCIANA—CAESALPINIA GILLIESII— FABACEAE The green seeds and sticky pods of this plant have caused severe gastrointestinal distress (Hodgson 2001). The leguminous seeds contain tannins that can be toxic, especially to pets and children. The leaves contain toxic hydrocyanic acid. The ornamental shrub occasionally grows wild in arid areas throughout the Southwest. The yellow flower petals do not have any other colors on them, but have prominent bright red stamens. Native Caesalpinia species may also be toxic. The pods of several species produce tannins useful for tanning and dyeing. For information on recognizing members of the legume family, see the index. BOXWOOD—BUXUS SEMPERVIRENS—BUXACEAE All parts of this evergreen shrub are toxic and are reported to have caused livestock deaths when consumed in large amounts. Grown ornamentally in the eastern U.S. BUTTERFLY WEED—ASCLEPIAS TUBEROSA—APOCYNACEAE See information on milkweeds above. Cardiac glycosides are highly toxic. Native to much of the U.S. CANDELABRAS CACTUS—EUPHORBIA LACTEA—EUPHORBIACEAE Not really a cactus at all, these succulent, spiny houseplants are somewhat toxic. The milky sap is an irritant. Refer to Euphorbia in the index for further details. CARNATION—DIANTHUS SPECIES—CARYOPHYLLACEAE Chewing the flowers can cause gastrointestinal distress. CASTOR BEAN—RICINUS COMMUNIS—EUPHORBIACEAE (See illustration, page 266.) All parts of the shrub are toxic, especially the seeds. One to three seeds can kill a child, but the hard seed coat may prevent intoxication. The plant can cause a skin rash in sensitive persons. Castor beans occasionally grow wild in the southern U.S. The toxic compounds include toxalbumins. See information above, and the index, for information on other members of this family. CHINABERRY—MELIA AZEDARACH—MELIACEAE All parts of the tree are toxic. Although berries of some plants have been eaten with no ill effects, as few as six to eight berries have killed a child. Berries can poison pets and have been used as an insecticide and fish poison. Chinaberry often grows wild in the southern U.S. (see the index for details). CENTURY PLANT—AGAVE AMERICANA—ASPARAGACEAE The leaves and roots are toxic and caustic. See Chapter 1, and the index for further information. See photograph, plate 6. CROCUS—CROCUS SPECIES—IRIDACEAE All parts of the garden flower are toxic, especially the bulbs. For further information on the iris family, see information above and the index. CROCUS, AUTUMN—COLCHICUM AUTUMNALE—COLCHICACEAE All parts of the garden flower are toxic, especially the bulbs. The toxic substance is called colchicine, which has had value in traditional medicine for thousands of years, and has been approved in the U.S. for treating gout. CYCAD, FALSE SAGO PALM—CYCAS SPECIES, ZAMIA SPECIES— CYCADACEAE AND ZAMIACEAE All parts of the palmlike shrubs are toxic to humans and pets. Paralysis and death have occurred from eating the fleshy fruits. CYCLAMEN—CYCLAMEN SPECIES—PRIMULACEAE Contains cyclamine, a saponin that is especially high in the roots, which can be deadly to pets and potentially highly toxic to humans. DAISY—CHRYSANTHEMUM SPECIES—ASTERACEAE Toxic to pets. Other plants commonly called daisies may not be toxic. Pyrethrum is an insecticide created from several species of Chrysanthemum. In China, the flowers of some species are used to make a mild tea and have been used in traditional medicine. DAPHNE—DAPHNE SPECIES—THYMELAEACEAE Has a highly toxic substance, daphnetoxin, a glycoside, particularly in the berries, leaves, and twigs. Also has mezerein, a skin irritant. Daphnetoxin is under study for its potential in medical applications, including treatment of cancer (Diogo, Felix, Vilela, Burgeiro, Barbosa, Carvalho, Oliveira, and Peixoto 2009). ELDERBERRY—SAMBUCUS SPECIES—CAPRIFOLIACEAE The green berries, leaves, twigs, and roots are somewhat toxic. Refer to index for details. (See illustration, page 201.) EUCALYPTUS—EUCALYPTUS SPECIES—MYRTACEAE Eucalyptus trees, native to Australia, are grown as ornamentals in Florida and California. Eucalyptus oil caused one human fatality in 2010. The oil from the leaves, cineole, is extracted for use in fragrances, as a flavoring, and for medicinal use. It is also used as an insect repellent. It can cause severe toxicity, especially in children. EYEBANE, SPURGE—EUPHORBIA MACULATA AND OTHER SPECIES —EUPHORBIACEAE Like its relative, poinsettia, listed below, this plant is only mildly toxic, but it can cause diarrhea and vomiting. FOUR-O’CLOCK—MIRABILIS JALAPA—NYCTAGINACEAE The roots and seeds of this flowering vining shrub have caused severe gastrointestinal distress in children. FOXGLOVE—DIGITALIS PURPUREA—PLANTAGINACEAE All parts of the garden flower are deadly poisonous. Children have been poisoned by drinking water from a vase that held the cut flowers. Foxglove is the source of numerous important medicinal cardiac glycosides and steroids. GOLDEN CHAIN TREE—LABURNUM ANAGYROIDES—FABACEAE This ornamental tree with hanging yellow flowers, found in some of the Pacific Coast states and Utah, has highly toxic leguminous seeds. All parts of the tree are considered toxic. The seeds can kill a child. The major toxin is the alkaloid cytisine, a nicotinic receptor agonist. It has caused death in livestock. HOLLY, YAUPON—ILEX SPECIES—AQUIFOLIACEAE (See illustration, page 163.) The red or black berries of the shrubs are toxic. Fatalities have occurred in children who consumed 20 to 30 berries. For further details, refer to the index. HYACINTH—HYACINTHUS ORIENTALIS—ASPARAGACEAE All parts of the garden flower are toxic, especially the bulb, and cause severe gastrointestinal distress. Sensitive individuals can contract a skin rash from the plant. For further information on toxic and edible members of the family, see the index. HYDRANGEA—HYDRANGEA SPECIES—HYDRANGEACEAE All parts of the shrub are toxic. The flower buds and leaves have caused human poisoning. IRIS—IRIS SPECIES—IRIDACEAE The bulb or rhizome and leaves of these garden flowers have caused poisoning. The unpleasant taste of the plant probably has prevented human fatalities. The plant can also cause a skin rash in sensitive individuals. For more details on the family, refer to information above and the index. IVY, ENGLISH IVY, NEEDLEPOINT IVY—HEDERA HELIX— ARALIACEAE Children have been seriously poisoned from the berries and leaves. English ivy can cause contact dermatitis in sensitive individuals. JAPANESE YEW—PODOCARPUS MACROPHYLLUS— PODOCARPACEAE The blue or purple berries of the evergreen shrub can cause severe gastrointestinal distress. Leaves have caused death in horses. Also refer to the red-fruited yew, Taxus, which has highly toxic seeds and leaves; see information below. JASMINE, CAROLINA JASMINE, YELLOW JESSAMINE, JAMÍN AMARILLO—GELSEMIUM SEMPERVIRENS—GELSEMIACEAE (See illustration, page 255.) All parts of this plant are deadly poisonous, including the flowers, and honey from the flowers is toxic. The plant also can cause a skin rash in sensitive persons. For illustration and details on this native vine, refer to the index. It is native to the southeastern U.S. into East Texas. JERUSALEM-CHERRY—SOLANUM PSEUDOCAPSICUM— SOLANACEAE The shrubby garden and pot plant contains highly toxic alkaloids in both the fruit and the leaves. The round yellow to red berries may be confused with those of chile pequín, which usually are not fully round (refer to the index for descriptions of both species). Jerusalem-cherry is grown ornamentally throughout the southeastern U.S. and occasionally may be found in the wild in Southeast Texas. JESSAMINE, JASMINE—CESTRUM SPECIES—SOLANACEAE JESSAMINE, JASMINE—CESTRUM SPECIES—SOLANACEAE All parts of the shrubs are highly toxic. Poisoning usually involves the attractive white, black, or purple berries. Both ripe and unripe berries can cause serious poisoning, paralysis, and death in humans and pets. For details about the three ornamental species, sometimes found wild in Texas, refer to the index, and see information above. JIMSONWEED—DATURA SPECIES—SOLANACEAE (See illustration, page 263.) All parts of the shrubby garden flower are highly toxic. Children have died from eating the seeds and leaves. The plant is well known for its hallucinogenic effects. A large percentage of attempts to get high from eating the seeds result in moderate to severe poisoning. The plant also can cause dermatitis in sensitive individuals. For further details on jimsonweed, see the index. JUNIPER—JUNIPERUS SPECIES—CUPRESSACEAE The berries (actually cones) are somewhat toxic. Refer to the index for details. See photo, plate 29. KENTUCKY COFFEE TREE—GYMNOCLADUS DIOICUS—FABACEAE The seeds and pods of this tree, native to the eastern half of North America, have caused severe poisoning. Though uncommon in North Texas landscapes, the tree may be confused with the honey locust (Gleditsia triacanthos), which has sweet pulp around its seeds. For descriptive information on both plants, see the index. LANTANA—LANTANA CAMARA, LANTANA URTICOIDES— VERBENACEAE (See illustration, page 182.) Unripe lantana fruit has caused deaths in children, pets, and livestock. The leaves and ripe fruit of the shrub are also toxic. Plant hairs can cause skin rash in sensitive individuals. For details, see the index. LARKSPUR, DELPHINIUM—DELPHINIUM SPECIES— RANUNCULACEAE All parts of the garden flower are deadly. The seeds, roots, and new growth contain the highest levels of toxic alkaloids. Severe poisoning and death can occur rapidly. Until recent years a lotion prepared from the seeds was used to treat head lice. The lotion was taken off the market because of human poisonings both from ingestion of the lotion and absorption through cuts in the skin. Wild species of larkspurs have caused much cattle loss in the West. LAUREL CHERRY—PRUNUS CAROLINIANA—ROSACEAE (See photograph, plate 57.) The leaves, bark, and seeds of the evergreen shrub or tree are highly toxic. The fruit is very astringent and inedible. The seeds and foliage of the edible cherry trees also can cause poisoning (refer to the index). LIGUSTRUM, PRIVET—LIGUSTRUM SPECIES—OLEACEAE (See photograph, plate 50.) The blue-black berries, leaves, and possibly other parts of these shrubs and trees are toxic to humans and livestock. Berries have caused fatalities in children. For a description of the plant, refer to the index. LILY OF THE VALLEY—CONVALLARIA MAJUSCULA— ASPARAGACEAE All parts of the garden flower are deadly poisonous, and the poison acts rapidly. The plants contain cardiac glycosides. Part of one leaf can cause death. For further information on toxic and edible members of the lily family, see the index. MAYAPPLE, MANDRAKE—PODOPHYLLUM PELTATUM— BERBERIDACEAE The leaves, roots, and green, unripe fruits are highly poisonous and can cause death. Refer to the index for details. See photograph, plate 17. MESCAL BEAN, TEXAS MOUNTAIN LAUREL, FRIJOLILLO—SOPHORA SECUNDIFLORA—FABACEAE (See photograph, plate 39.) All parts of the shrub or tree are toxic to humans and livestock. A single seed can potentially kill an adult, though the hard seed coat usually prevents poisoning. Though Warnock (1970) reports that flowers brought into the house can cause nausea, the Texas State Poison Center has had no reports of such problems. For further information on this native bean, see the index. MISTLETOE—PHORADENDRON SPECIES—SANTALACEAE All parts of this Christmas ornament are highly toxic. Deaths have occurred in children and adults from eating the berries and drinking a tea of them. The plant can cause a skin rash in sensitive persons. For illustration and details about mistletoe, see the index. See illustration, page 187. MONKSHOOD—ACONITUM SPECIES—RANUNCULACEAE All parts of the garden flower are highly toxic. Convulsions and death can occur in a few hours. MOONFLOWER—IPOMOEA SPECIES—CONVOLVULACEAE MORNING GLORY—IPOMOEA VIOLACEA—CONVOLVULACEAE Seeds of these vines consumed accidentally by children or for hallucinogenic effects by adults can cause nausea and psychotic reactions. Packaged seeds are treated with toxic insecticides. Indigenous tribes in the Sonoran Desert have used the roots of several Mexican species for food in the past: Ipomoea jicama (not to be confused with the agricultural jicama) and I. bracteata; and the Tarahumaras ate the boiled leaves and flowers of I. purpurea (Hodgson 2001). MUSHROOMS More humans die annually from eating mushrooms than from any flowering plants. Mushrooms caused three human fatalities in the U.S. in 2010 (AAPC annual report). The mushrooms implicated were Amanita pantherina, Russula fragilis, and Gymnopilus spectabilis. Refer to Chapter 6 for more information on mushrooms. NANDINA—NANDINA DOMESTICA—BERBERIDACEAE Cats have been poisoned by the red berries of the shrub. Michael Ellis (interview, January 1986) reports that in humans the only toxic symptom on record is a mildly upset stomach. OLEANDER—NERIUM OLEANDER—APOCYNACEAE (See illustration, page 266.) All parts of the shrub are deadly, including the flowers. Honey from nectar may also be toxic. A single leaf is considered lethal to an adult. The plant contains cardiac glycosides. Near deaths have occurred when children made a tea of the leaves and flowers. Though one source (Kinghorn 1979) says that smoke from burning the shrubs may be toxic, the Texas State Poison Center has no reported cases. Refer above for further information on the family, and see the index. OXALIS, WOOD SORREL—OXALIS SPECIES—OXALIDACEAE While most species can be eaten in small quantities, the plants contain oxalic acid, which can cause gastrointestinal distress in quantity, particularly in pets. Refer to the index for further information. See illustration, page 119. PEACE LILY, SPATHE FLOWER—SPATHIPHYLLUM SPECIES— ARACEAE One of the most frequent causes of poisoning in children. See further information on the family above, or refer to the index. PERIWINKLE—VINCA SPECIES—APOCYNACEAE The dried leaves of this ground cover have been smoked for a hallucinogenic effect, but the plant contains toxic alkaloids and can cause kidney and liver damage if large amounts are consumed. Compounds derived from Vinca are used in medicines for treating childhood leukemia and Hodgkin’s disease (Lewis and Elvin-Lewis 1977). See information on the family above. PIGEONBERRY, ADONIS MORADO, DEWDROPS—DURANTA ERECTA —VERBENACEAE The yellow fruit of this ornamental shrub, grown in the South, has caused death in children. Refer to the index for other toxic members of the family. PITTOSPORUM—PITTOSPORUM SPECIES—PITTOSPORACEAE These plants contain toxic saponins. Poisoning usually involves the berries and can cause severe gastrointestinal distress. POINSETTIA—EUPHORBIA PULCHERRIMA—EUPHORBIACEAE Despite rumors otherwise, the ornamental Christmas plant, poinsettia, is only mildly toxic. All parts of the houseplant can cause gastrointestinal distress, but no reports of death in humans have been verified. The plant can cause diarrhea and vomiting. And the sap can cause dermatitis in some individuals and can cause temporary blindness if rubbed into the eyes. The diterpene esters in the milky latex cause the irritation. See information on other members of the family, above and in the index. PYRACANTHA, FIRE-THORN—PYRACANTHA SPECIES—ROSACEAE The raw berries, especially when green and unripe, are mildly irritating to the stomach. The nasty thorns can cause dermatitis in some individuals. But the cooked berries can be made into jelly. Refer to the index for further details. SANSEVIERIA, MOTHER-IN-LAW’S-TONGUE—SANSEVIERIA SPECIES —ASPARAGACEAE Though rarely causing toxicity in humans, the houseplant has caused deaths of dogs and is potentially toxic to humans. Refer to the index for information on other members of the family. SCHEFFLERA—SCHEFFLERA SPECIES—ARALIACEAE Dogs have died from eating the houseplant. The 1982 NCPCC report indicates several cases of toxic ingestion by children. For further details on the family, see the index. STRING OF PEARLS—SENECIO ROWLEYANUS—ASTERACEAE All parts of this African succulent houseplant are toxic. The plant produces hanging strings of pearl-shaped leaves. SWEETPEA—LATHYRUS LATIFOLIUS, LATHYRUS ODORATUS, AND OTHER SPECIES—FABACEAE Ingestion of a large number of the seeds of this garden vine can cause paralysis and convulsions. For details, see the index. TALLOW TREE, CHINESE TALLOW TREE—TRIADICA SEBIFERA— EUPHORBIACEAE The green to blackish fruit of the tree has caused severe gastrointestinal distress, although there are no records of poisoning from the hard white seeds. The leaves and white milky sap are also toxic and irritant. See the index for a description of the plant. TREE-OF-HEAVEN—AILANTHUS ALTISSIMA—SIMAROUBACEAE The leaves are toxic if eaten and also can cause dermatitis. TULIPS—TULIPA SPECIES—LILIACEAE All parts are toxic, particularly the bulbs. They are particularly toxic for pets. They contain cardiac glycosides. Humans have been poisoned by confusing the bulbs with edible lilies. For further information on the lily family, see information above or the index. TUNG OIL TREE—VERNICIA FORDII—EUPHORBIACEAE All parts of the tree are toxic. Poisoning usually occurs from confusing this nut with a brazil nut. One to three seeds can produce severe illness. Fatalities are rare, though, and recovery is usually rapid. The plant can cause dermatitis in sensitive individuals. Tung oil trees have been planted in the southeastern U.S., into Southeast Texas. See the index for information on other members of the family. VIOLET—VIOLA SPECIES—VIOLACEAE While the flowers and leaves are edible, the seeds and roots are toxic and can cause severe gastrointestinal distress. Warning, yellow violet flowers tend to be laxative. African violets, Saintpaulia species, are not related and are not toxic, but they also are not considered edible. VIRGINIA CREEPER—PARTHENOCISSUS QUINQUEFOLIA— VITACEAE The blue-black berries, which can be mistaken for grapes, are highly toxic. The high oxalic acid content has caused human fatalities. Refer to the index for further details. See illustration, page 184. WISTERIA—WISTERIA SPECIES—FABACEAE (See photograph, plate 10.) Pods, flowers, and seeds of the vine are toxic. One or two seeds can cause severe gastrointestinal distress in children. No human fatalities have been reported. See the index for a description. YESTERDAY-TODAY-AND-TOMORROW—BRUNFELSIA SPECIES— SOLANACEAE Dogs have died from eating this houseplant. Consider it potentially toxic to humans. For further information on the family, see above, and refer to the index. YEW, PACIFIC YEW—TAXUS BREVIFOLIA—TAXACEAE This red-fruited evergreen shrub has highly toxic seeds and leaves. It is native to the Pacific Coast from California to Alaska. There is also a native yew in Florida, Taxus floridana. Canada yew, Taxus canadensis, is native to the northeastern part of North America. A Japanese yew, Taxus cuspidata, is grown in the northeastern states. All species should be considered toxic. Also refer to Japanese yew, listed above. POISONS IN THE GARDEN AND THE KITCHEN Information on poisonous plants would be incomplete without mention of the potential poisons in our vegetable gardens, on our fruit trees, and in our kitchens. We take for granted that the fruits and vegetables we eat daily can do us no harm. And yet many people have an allergic reaction when they eat certain types of foods—peanuts, milk, eggs, wheat, and corn, to name a few. And some people develop an allergic dermatitis after handling certain fruits and vegetables.
